## P4.3 The `citar-helper` crate

### P4.3.1 Structure and dependencies

- **Crate:** `crates/citar-helper`, a library (`citar_helper`, so tests drive it in-process) and the binary `citar-helper`. Modules: `cli`, `config`, `home` (the directory layout), `log` (redacting), `session` (the WebSocket, handshake, reconnect, dispatch of frames), `protocol` (every frame as a serde type), `llm` (discovery, catalog, the proxy), `scan` (hardware and power), `jobs` (the job host's parent side, signing), `key`, `update`, `service`, `quiet`.
- **The crate graph** (`xtask/src/check/graph.rs`): `citar-jobs: [citar-engine, citar-bot, citar-sim]`; `citar-helper: [citar-engine, citar-bot, citar-sim, citar-jobs]`; `citar-py` gains `citar-jobs`. `citar-store` is not needed by the helper (jobs keep no saves) and stays off its row until something needs it.
- **Dependencies** (workspace entries, each with its reason in the manifest): `tokio` (rt-multi-thread, net, time, process, io-util, sync, signal, fs), `tokio-tungstenite` with rustls, `reqwest` with rustls and streaming, `rustls-platform-verifier` (the OS's own trust store: no CA bundle hunt as in `agent.py:58-87`, no OpenSSL), `serde`, `serde_json`, `clap`, `ed25519-dalek` and `getrandom` (in citar-jobs), `blake3` (workspace), `sysinfo`, `base64`, `semver`, `chrono` (local quiet hours), `thiserror`, `self-replace` (the update's swap on every OS), and per OS `windows-service` and `windows-sys` (Windows), `libc` (Unix). No C toolchain beyond what rustls' crypto backend builds with.
- **`unsafe`:** the workspace keeps `unsafe_code = "deny"`. The only `unsafe` in Phase 4 is in `crates/citar-jobs/src/sys/{windows,unix}.rs`, the OS calls no safe wrapper makes (Job Objects; `setpriority`, `setrlimit`; on Windows the owner-only DACLs of P4.3.7 and P4.3.9), each block with a `// SAFETY:` comment, allowed for that module alone; `cargo xtask check` gains a rule refusing an `unsafe` token anywhere else in `citar-jobs` and anywhere in `citar-helper`, which calls the module's safe functions.
- **Features:** `test-faults` (P4.4.6), `test-update-key` (P4.3.8), `test-jobs` in citar-jobs (the `burn` and `alloc` job kinds the limit tests use) are test features; `dev-guard` is the development build's guard, which `cargo xtask develop` (and so every helper the packages and QA run) always sets: loopback addresses only, and no start without an explicit `--home` outside the platform's per-user base (P4.3.2); `packaged` is Phase 5's, for the tap, bucket and winget builds (P4.3.8). `cargo xtask check` refuses the test features and `dev-guard` in citar-py's dependencies except as a forward of citar-py's own `test-ops`, in `pyproject.toml`'s maturin features, and on any `cargo build` line of `release.yml` and `helper.yml`'s artifact job. **A helper built with a test feature or `dev-guard` dials loopback addresses only** (the server and the update URL; compile-time), so no development or test build can reach a real server; release builds are made only by the workflows.

### P4.3.2 Configuration, compatibility and isolation

- **The home.** `--home DIR` (or `CITAR_DATA_DIR`, as Python) is the per-user base: `helper.json` at its root (v1's place), and the helper's own state under `helper/`: `machine-key.json`, `logs/helper.log` (rotated at 5 MB, three kept), `update/` (staging), `scan.json` (the last scan and its hash), `results/` (unacknowledged results, P4.2.5). Without either, the platform's per-user base (P4.0), which on this laptop also holds the owner's `citar.db`, `secret_key` and `config/`: a `dev-guard` build (P4.3.1) therefore refuses to start without an explicit `--home` outside that base, so a forgotten flag fails instead of writing there. The helper writes nothing outside its home (and the service definitions P4.3.9 writes on request).
- **Compatibility.** Every v1 way of configuring keeps working with the same meaning: `helper.json`, `--config worker.json` (any path), the `CITAR_WORKER_*` variables, every v1 flag, the quiet-hours syntax, the first-run prompt on a TTY (server, token, "remember these in …?"), "Press Enter to close" when a double-clicked binary fails. 4-03's gate 1 holds this against a table recorded from `citar/worker/__main__.py:build_config` before 4-05 deletes it.
- **New settings** (flags and `helper.json` keys): `--no-llm`; `cpu: {enabled, threads, cpu_percent, memory_mb, work}`, the local switch and ceiling. **CPU lending is off by default**, so it is the lender's decision on the machine itself and not only on the site (where an administrator, or a compromised server, could otherwise turn it on): `setup` asks "Lend this machine's spare CPU to the server? [y/N]" and writes `cpu.enabled`; `--cpu` turns it on for one run; turned on without figures, the ceiling is half the logical CPUs, 50% and a quarter of the RAM, every kind. A 0.1.5 `helper.json` has no `cpu` key, so a migrated helper lends no CPU until its owner says so, and the site's dialog then says "not lent by this machine's owner". `updates: auto|notify|off` (default `auto`, `notify` in a `packaged` build, P4.3.8); `stream: true|false` (default true); `--ca-file FILE` (trust a server's own CA, the right answer to a self-signed server); `--no-power`. `--insecure` is kept for compatibility but honoured only for loopback and private (RFC 1918, ULA, `.local`) hosts, with a warning.
- **Subcommands:** `run` (the default; `--service`, which every service definition passes, and on Windows `--detach`, P4.3.9), `setup` (the first-run questions, also to replace a token), `scan [FILE]` (print or write the hardware report: the standalone collector), `service install|uninstall|status|run` (P4.3.9), `update check|apply`, `job-host` (internal: the job host, P4.4.3), `--self-test` (P4.4.3, P4.3.8), `--version` (version, build id, jobs build, target, protocol, and the key's fingerprint when its home holds a key).
- **Isolation in tests** is a rule (P4.7.1): every test and QA run passes `--home` to a fresh directory under its own scratch or QA directory, and sets `CITAR_DATA_DIR` to it as well.

### P4.3.3 The connection

- A single tokio runtime. The session task owns the socket: it reads frames, routes each to its handler (requests to the proxy, jobs to the job side, settings to the limits), and writes through one channel, so a slow handler never blocks pings. Scans, key generation and file reads run on `spawn_blocking`.
- **Reconnect:** 2 s doubling to 60 s with 30% jitter (v1's), reset by a welcome; the `refused` and `shutdown` reactions of P4.2.2 and P4.2.7; a TLS failure is logged with the host and the verifier's reason, never relaxed.
- **On each connect:** discover models (P4.3.4), read or scan hardware (cached; P4.3.5), send `hello` with `running` and `results`, then follow the welcome's `jobs`.
- **On a lost connection:** every completion in flight is cancelled at once (P4.2.4); job runs go on, and their results wait in `helper/results/`.
- **Local policy, enforced here whatever the server asks** (v1's principle, kept): the model allowlist, the local slots, the local quiet hours (no new request or job starts; running jobs finish), the CPU ceiling, the update policy.

### P4.3.4 The LLM proxy

- **Endpoints:** `lmstudio` (`/api/v0/models` for load state and context, else `/v1/models`), `ollama` (`/v1/models`, and `/api/ps` for what is loaded), `openai_compatible`, `openai` (a key from `api_key_env`, read locally, sent only to the configured base URL). The configured base URL and key are the only ones the helper ever dials for completions.
- **Streaming by default:** `stream: true` with `stream_options.include_usage`; the helper assembles content, reasoning (`reasoning_content` or `reasoning`, whichever the endpoint uses), and tool calls from their deltas (by index: id, name, arguments concatenated), and keeps `finish_reason` and the final usage. `stream: false` (or an endpoint that refuses streaming with tools, detected once and remembered for the connection) sends one non-streaming request; its cancel then closes the connection as well, but whether the endpoint stops is its own business (logged). Streaming is chosen for cancel, not for the server: the server still receives one `done` (P4.1.3).
- **Real cancel:** each request is a task holding its response stream; `cancel` aborts the task, which drops the stream and closes the TCP connection (reqwest never reuses a connection whose body was not read to the end); `cancelled` is sent after that, with the tokens received so far. A lost connection to the server aborts every request task the same way.
- **Slots and refusals:** `busy` beyond the local slots, `closed` in local quiet hours, `unknown_model` outside the allowlist, `timeout` when `timeout_s` (capped at 900 s locally) passes, `endpoint` for a connection or HTTP error from the endpoint (with its status and the first 300 characters of its body).
- **Timing:** `gen_s` and `ttft_s` from `Instant`; the server bounds them by what it observed (P3.3.4, unchanged).

### P4.3.5 The hardware scan and power samples

- **`scan`** produces the `citar-hardware` format `hwinfo.collect()` produces (same keys; `collector_version` 2, `collector: "citar-helper"`), so 3-18's `valid_scan`, `clean_hardware`, classes and the dialog read it unchanged: `sysinfo` for the OS, CPU model, cores, threads, RAM, disks and volumes; GPUs from `nvidia-smi --query-gpu` (names, memory, power limit, whether power is readable), on Windows `Get-CimInstance Win32_VideoController` through PowerShell (as `hwinfo.py` does, so the logic and the answers match), on Linux `lspci` and `rocm-smi`, on macOS `system_profiler SPDisplaysDataType SPHardwareDataType -json` and unified memory; the form (a battery means a laptop); runtimes (LM Studio and Ollama answering locally); `power.suggested` from a port of `guess_power`. Every probe is best-effort with a timeout, as in `hwinfo.py`: less information, never an error. **Probes are injectable for tests:** in a `dev-guard` or test build only, `CITAR_HELPER_NVIDIA_SMI`, `CITAR_HELPER_POWERSHELL` and `CITAR_HELPER_SYSTEM_PROFILER` name the probe programs, so a test runs a fake (a small test binary, an `.exe` on Windows). A fake first on `PATH` would not do: on Windows `Command` searches System32 before the parent's `PATH`, and the NVIDIA driver installs the real `nvidia-smi.exe` there (this laptop has one).
- **Auto or manual, per section, is the site's choice** (3-18, unchanged): the helper always reports, the machine's hardware, models and power modes decide what a report may change, and the last report is always kept for "Apply". The helper's own manual is to report a file or nothing.
- **When:** at connect (a cached scan younger than 6 hours is reused), every 6 hours, and on `rescan`; a `hardware` frame only when the scan's hash changed. `--hardware FILE` reports the file instead (read again at each connect, UTF-16 accepted, v1's rule); `--no-hardware` reports nothing.
- **Power:** every 10 s, `nvidia-smi --query-gpu=power.draw` where power is readable and the CPU utilisation from `sysinfo`; one `power` frame a minute. `--no-power` turns it off. Machines without a readable GPU power report CPU utilisation only, and costing keeps using their figures for the rest.

**As built in 4-06: the hardware scan and power samples in Rust** (P4.3.5, P4.5.7's power rows, P4.5.11's "Rescan now"; Appendix E). One agent, 2026-10-15, from 06:05 to 09:03 (-04:00), on branch `p-4-06` from `0e4cc53`. Rust (the helper crate: `src/scan/`, `src/power.rs`, the session's report and meter, the `scan` command, a second binary), Python (the hub's power rows, the hub's player, tests, both QA scripts), JS (the dialog's button, Live sampling for helper machines), the docs. The build id is unchanged (`0b412a18fe95`; sysinfo's `disk` feature adds no package to the lock).

- **The scan** (`src/scan/mod.rs` 580 lines with its tests, `windows.rs` 267, `linux.rs` 332, `macos.rs` 220, `guess.rs` 204, `probe.rs` 100; `src/power.rs` 172): the `citar-hardware` format `hwinfo.collect` writes, `collector_version` 2 and `collector` `citar-helper`. `sysinfo` stands in for the collector's `psutil` (the host name; the OS as Python's `platform` names it; the CPU's name, physical cores and logical threads; the memory; the volumes, Linux keeping only device file systems as `psutil.disk_partitions(all=False)` does); `nvidia-smi --query-gpu=...` read as `_nvidia` reads it; then each OS's part read as the collector reads it, each parser compiled on every OS so its tests run everywhere and only its `probe` on its own: Windows the same PowerShell CIM query (asking for UTF-8 output first), Linux `/proc/cpuinfo`, `/proc/meminfo`, DMI, RAPL, the CPU frequency policies, `rocm-smi`, `lspci`, `lsblk` and a battery, macOS `sysctl` and `system_profiler`. Python's semantics are kept where they decide a figure: truthiness, `a or b`, `round(x, n)` (Rust's exact formatting: 1.25 to 1.2, 2.675 to 2.67), `_gb` and the GPU merge. `guess_power` is ported with half-to-even rounding, the GPUs summed in order as floats, and the laptop CPU pattern matched by hand (no regex crate). Every probe runs with a timeout, standard input closed, standard error dropped and on Windows no console window (`CREATE_NO_WINDOW`); a probe that is missing, fails or overruns gives less, never an error. The scan is held to 32 GPUs, 64 disks, 64 volumes, 64 memory modules, 8 runtimes of 500 models and texts of 1,000 characters, and a scan over 256 KiB as sent is not reported. On this laptop a scan takes 2.4 s, PowerShell's query 2.2 of it.
- **Its runtimes:** the helper's own endpoint, when it is LM Studio or Ollama and its listings answered, with the keys and context lengths of the models its last listing named, within the owner's allowlist (`scan::runtime_models`; the fix round's: not the catalog's models, which add an allowlisted model the endpoint does not list). **Decision:** not the collector's `lms ls --json` and `ollama list`: P4.3.10 lets the helper dial only its server and its configured endpoint, and the design's words were "LM Studio and Ollama answering locally". The standalone `citar-helper scan` reads the endpoint, `models` and `no_llm` from the helper's own settings as `run` does (`config::endpoint`: the flags, then `--config` or the home's `helper.json`, then `CITAR_WORKER_*`; a development build reads a home only when `--home` names one, outside the per-user folder), asks that endpoint for 5 s, and writes nothing but its `FILE`.
- **Fixed on the way** (the collector's, not carried): `rocm-smi`'s and `lspci`'s GPUs were merged with `nvidia-smi`'s together, so an AMD card both list came out twice; the Rust scan merges `rocm-smi`'s first and `lspci`'s against both. A Mac's GPU memory that does not read as a number (`float("1.2.3")`) lost the whole macOS part to the collector's `except`; here it is left out alone.
- **When** (`src/session.rs`, P4.3.5): a scan younger than 6 hours by the wall clock serves the hello, kept across connections and across runs in `helper/scan.json` (`{v, at, hash, scan}`, written beside and renamed over; refused when it is not this collector's, does not hash to its own `hash`, is 6 hours old, was written "after now", or, the fix round's, was made before the machine last started, `Clock::boot_unix`, since hardware changes with the machine off); an older one or none is made before the hello, which waits for it 30 s at most and otherwise goes with the last scan it has (or none) while the scan's `hardware` frame follows. The machine is scanned again every 6 hours and a `hardware` frame goes only when the scan's hash changed from what the server has (the hash leaves out `collected_at`, each GPU's `power_now_w` and each volume's `free_gb`); a `rescan` sends one whatever the scan holds. Scans run on `spawn_blocking`, never on the session's task, and the scan's task also checks its size, hashes it and keeps it in `helper/scan.json` (at most 5 s), so the session's loop waits on no disk. A scan's time and the next one's are Unix times on the helper's clock, read as tokio's time only for the wait: tokio's clock counts from the machine's start (and stops while Linux sleeps), so a kept scan's age has no place on it. `--hardware FILE` (UTF-16 accepted) and `--no-hardware` are as in 4-03: a file is read at each connection and on `rescan`, never on its own; nothing is never scanned, and a `rescan` then sends nothing (logged).
- **Power** (`src/power.rs`): a reading every 10 s on a task of the connection's own (`nvidia-smi --query-gpu=power.draw`, summed as `PowerSampler._gpu_watts` reads it; the CPU's utilisation from `sysinfo` since the last reading), sent to the session's loop, and one `power` frame after each sixth reading with the readings that measured something (none, and a log line an hour, when none did). Where the driver reports no draw, `nvidia-smi` is asked again an hour later; one that answered is given a minute of misses first. `--no-power` starts no meter. **Decision:** the meter belongs to the connection: a partial minute when the connection ends is not sent later (the protocol would carry up to 360 samples; a helper not connected is serving nothing).
- **Probes injectable** (P4.3.5): in a `DEV_GUARD` build only, `CITAR_HELPER_NVIDIA_SMI`, `CITAR_HELPER_POWERSHELL` and `CITAR_HELPER_SYSTEM_PROFILER` name the probe programs (`Probes::from_env`; the helper logs each one named). **`citar-fake-probe`** (`src/bin/fake_probe.rs`, 63 lines) is a second binary of the crate with `required-features = ["dev-guard"]`, so no release build (no features) ever holds it; `default-run = "citar-helper"` keeps `cargo run -p citar-helper` the helper. It prints fixed answers (one 24 GB "CITAR Fake GPU" with a 300 W limit drawing 123.45 W) and logs each call, one line with its arguments, to `CITAR_FAKE_PROBE_LOG` or beside itself. `cargo xtask develop` and test.yml's build-ext job put it beside the helper.
- **The hub** (`citar/server/workers.py`): `power` leaves `NOT_YET`. Each frame is one power row of the machine (`record_power` in a thread, `usage.append`'s `power-<machine>-YYYY-MM.jsonl`), **placed by the hub's clock, never the samples' `t`** (the helper's clock is not the server's): it ends when the frame came and covers 10 s a sample, never before the connection's start or the machine's last frame, across its connections (`WorkerHub.power_since`, the fix round's: the first pass kept this per connection, so a helper that reconnected and sent samples at once wrote a row over its last one), and a frame leaving under 10 s writes none; a frame sooner than 30 s after the machine's last one is dropped (logged once); a draw over 20 kW, or over 1.5 times the power limits the helper's own scan lists for the GPUs whose draw reads (`gpu_draw_cap`, when it lists one for each), is left out; a machine whose `power.sampling` is `off` gets none. **Costing** (the fix round's: the first pass said it needed no change, wrongly): a row without `gpu_w` (a machine whose GPU's draw does not read: an AMD or Intel GPU, a Mac, as on the owner's Framework Desktop) had the minute priced from the CPU alone and called measured; each part is now the samples' where they read it and estimated as in a minute without samples where not (`costing.compute`), a minute is measured only when no part of it was estimated, and the metered calendar leaves out rows that did not read a part the machine's power figures price. The same held for the host's own sampler on a machine without an NVIDIA card (Phase 3). The Servers page's Hardware & power editor now offers **Live sampling** for helper machines too.
- **The dialog's "Rescan now"** (`machine_settings.js`, `machines.rescanned`): on the second tab, for a machine's manager; it asks the route, then reads the dialog again every 0.5 s for up to 15 s until the last report's time moves, and shows the new report; a helper not connected is the route's 409, said as it says it.
- **The transcripts:** the helper plays 49 (4-03's 31, 4-04's 15 and 4-06's three, with a fixed scanner and meter in-process on the paused clock), the hub 43 (4-05's 40 and the three). `HubPlayer` gains the states `last_report` (the machine's report time moves past the hello's) and `power_rows`, and moves its fake clock a keep-alive interval at a time through a long wait while the helper is connected (`hardware-unchanged`'s six hours in about two seconds rather than seven minutes). **Fixture change:** `power-minute`'s hub setup takes the fake clock, so the hub's side does not wait a real minute.

### P4.3.6 CPU jobs on the helper

- **The job host is a child process,** `citar-helper job-host` (P4.4.3), started when CPU work is enabled (site and local switch both), with the effective limits (P4.2.3), and restarted between jobs when the limits change. It reports its `jobs_build` in `ready`, and the parent refuses (and restarts) a host whose build differs from its own, so a host left from before a binary swap never computes with the old code (P4.3.8). Its standard input and output carry the job frames; the helper relays them to and from the server, checks them, and signs results (P4.3.7). Running the games in a child keeps a crash, an out-of-memory kill or a hung game away from the connection, and gives every OS one process to put its limits on.
- **How each OS holds the limits** (C3: "hard where the OS allows"):

| OS | CPU (`cpu_percent` of the whole machine) | Memory (`memory_mb`, all jobs together) | Reported |
|---|---|---|---|
| Windows | the job host puts itself in a Job Object with `JOB_OBJECT_CPU_RATE_CONTROL_ENABLE` and `HARD_CAP`, `CpuRate = percent x 100`, at `BELOW_NORMAL_PRIORITY_CLASS` | the same Job Object's `JobMemoryLimit`, with `KILL_ON_JOB_CLOSE` | hard, hard |
| Linux with a reachable user manager | the parent starts it under `systemd-run --user --scope --quiet --collect -p CPUQuota=<percent x CPUs>% -p MemoryMax=<MB>M -p TasksMax=<threads + 16>`; the job host reads its own cgroup's `cpu.max` and `memory.max` to confirm | as left | hard, hard |
| Linux otherwise | `nice 10` and the thread cap only | `RLIMIT_DATA` (private writable memory: `RLIMIT_AS` would count the address space glibc reserves per thread) | soft, hard |
| macOS | background priority (`PRIO_DARWIN_BG`, efficiency cores on Apple silicon) and the thread cap | a watchdog in the job host reads its resident size every 500 ms and ends the jobs past the limit | soft, soft |

- **`threads`** is the most games at once (one thread each); the server never offers more, and the host refuses more (`busy`).
- **`yield_to_llm`** (a site setting, default on): while any model request of this helper is in flight, the job host is told `pause` and stops every game at its next seat step (each well under a second), then `resume`. A GPU machine lending its spare CPU does not slow its own model. **Benchmarks:** while a benchmark lease is live on the machine, the server offers it no CPU job and sends `jobs_pause` (whatever `yield_to_llm` says), then `jobs_resume` when the lease ends (P4.5.5), so running games never contaminate a benchmark's timing.
- **Deadlines:** the helper aborts a run past its `deadline_s` itself (`job_failed` `deadline`), not counting paused time, up to twice the deadline in all; it reports `paused_s`, and the server's own timeout, extended the same way, is only the backstop.
- **Reconnects:** a run continues across a lost connection; the next `hello` lists it in `running`, and the welcome says whether to keep it. A result is written to `helper/results/` before it is sent and kept there until its `job_result_ack`, so one produced while disconnected, or sent just before a connection died, is sent again after the reconnect if the welcome's `jobs.resend` asks for it (P4.2.5).

### P4.3.7 Keys and signing

- **The machine key** is an ed25519 key generated on first run from the OS's random source (`getrandom`), stored in `helper/machine-key.json` (`{"v": 1, "public": base64, "secret": base64}`), written with mode 0600 on Unix and, on Windows, with an owner-only DACL (its owner, SYSTEM and Administrators; protected from inheritance; set through citar-jobs' `sys` module, P4.3.1). **At start the helper refuses a key file anyone else can read:** group or others on Unix, as SSH does; any other principal in its DACL on Windows, where a `--home` such as `C:\citar` inherits Authenticated Users' modify rights and anything under `%ProgramData%` inherits Users' read. A `helper.json` (it holds the token) that others can read gets a warning and the command that fixes it. The secret never leaves the machine: it is not in any frame, log or scan.
- **Registration:** the first v2 hello of a machine registers its public key on the row (trust on first use, bound to a valid token); a later hello with another key is refused (`key`) until the owner or an admin resets the machine's key (audited, P4.5.1). A token issued again keeps the key. A key reset never changes the machine's trust (P4.5.8): a quarantined machine stays quarantined with a new key. The Servers page shows the key's fingerprint (the first 16 hex of blake3 of the public key), which `citar-helper --version` also prints, so an owner can check it is their machine.
- **Signing:** the helper signs each claim the job host's outcome yields (P4.4.4) before relaying the result. Signing in the parent keeps the key away from the job host, which runs the games.

### P4.3.8 Self-update

- **The manifest** (`citar-helper-manifest.json`): `{v: 1, version, protocol, build, released_at, assets: {<target>: {name, url, sha256, size}}}`, the five targets of P4.10. It is signed as a whole: `citar-helper-manifest.json.sig` = ed25519 over `"CITAR-HELPER-MANIFEST-V1\0" ‖ the manifest's bytes as published`. Both files are release assets beside the binaries.
- **Trusted keys:** the helper embeds the public keys in `crates/citar-jobs/update-keys.pub` (one base64 key per line, up to two, for rotation). **In Phase 4 that file is empty:** no real key exists yet, and a build that trusts no key reports updates `unavailable` and never downloads anything. Phase 5 adds the owner's public key (the owner question on custody, P4.10). Only a build with `test-update-key` also trusts a key given at run time (`--update-key FILE`), and only such a build reads `CITAR_HELPER_VERSION` at compile time to call itself another version.
- **Where the manifest comes from:** the server relays it (`welcome.update` and `update`); the helper does not poll GitHub. The server fetches the manifest **of its own version** (`helper_manifest_url`, default `https://github.com/jprodgers/CITAR/releases/download/v{version}/citar-helper-manifest.json` and its `.sig`; or `helper_manifest_file` for tests), at most hourly, off any request path, and relays it only if it verifies against the same keys (P4.5.9). Helpers therefore follow the server they serve: same engine build, same protocol.
- **The helper's steps,** under its local policy (`off`: ignore; `notify`: report `available` and stop there; `auto`):
  1. verify the signature against the trusted keys; refuse otherwise (`update_status refused`);
  2. refuse unless `version` is newer than its own (no downgrade, so a server cannot push an old signed build with a known flaw), and unless the manifest has its target;
  3. refuse a URL that is not `https`, or `http` to a loopback host (tests);
  4. download into `helper/update/` and check `size` and `sha256`, following redirects only to `https` URLs (at most five: GitHub serves release assets from another host); the integrity is the signed hash and size, never the host;
  5. run the staged binary with `--self-test` (it starts as far as loading its embedded ruleset, running the job canary and the self-check of P4.4.3, then prints its version, build, jobs build and target, which must equal the manifest's): the guard against a binary that cannot run, or computes differently, on this machine;
  6. wait until idle (no request or job in flight; at most an hour, then between two jobs);
  7. **the hand-off:** the old process stops taking work, stops its job host, sends `update_status restarting`, closes its socket, writes `helper/update/pending` (the old and new versions, a start count), and swaps the binary with `self-replace`, keeping the old one as `<name>.old`. Then:
     - **a console run** starts the new binary as its child and becomes its **supervisor**: it waits on the child and exits with the child's code. If the child exits before `helper/update/healthy` exists, the supervisor restores `.old`, runs it in the child's place, and that process reports `failed`.
     - **a service run** (`--service`, or under `run --detach`'s supervisor) exits with code 75, and its supervisor or service manager starts the new binary (P4.3.9). Rollback is the new binary's own start path: it reads `pending` first thing, counts the start, and on the third start without `healthy` restores `.old` and exits 75, so the manager starts the old binary, which reports `failed`.
     - The new binary writes `helper/update/healthy` after its first welcome, or after 60 s of running while the server cannot be reached, deletes `pending`, and reports `applied`. `replaced` is never the success signal: the old process closed its socket before the new one dialled. `citar-helper update rollback` restores `.old` by hand.
- **The job host follows the binary:** it is stopped at step 7, and a host whose `jobs_build` differs from its parent's is refused (P4.3.6).
- **Packaged builds** (Phase 5's Homebrew tap, Scoop bucket and winget package) are built with the `packaged` feature: `updates` defaults to `notify`, and `auto` is refused with "update through your package manager", so the helper never fights the package manager over its own binary. The GitHub release assets are built without it.
- **Who signs** is Phase 5's (P4.10): `cargo xtask keygen --out DIR` (refuses a directory inside the repository), `cargo xtask helper-manifest --assets DIR --version V` and `cargo xtask sign-manifest --key FILE MANIFEST` (refuses a key file inside the repository or known to git) exist from 4-13, so whichever custody the owner chooses has its tools. (xtask signs with `ed25519-dalek` directly; its row of the crate graph stays empty, and the bytes it signs are the manifest file as published, so no shared type is needed.)

### P4.3.9 Service installation

`citar-helper service install|uninstall|status|run [--user|--system] [--dry-run]`. Every definition runs the helper with `--service`, an explicit `--home` and the absolute path of the binary; `--dry-run` prints what would be written and run, and changes nothing.

**Exit codes are part of the contract:** 0, stopped by the user or the service manager; 75, restart for an update (P4.3.8); 78, a permanent refusal (P4.2.2, P4.2.7), from a console run only; anything else, a crash. **In service mode a permanent refusal does not exit:** the helper idles, logs once, and tries again hourly, because launchd cannot exclude an exit code from `KeepAlive` and a helper restarted every 15 s would retry a revoked token forever (and, before P4.5.1's fix, lock its neighbours out of the rate limit).

| OS | `--user` (default where possible) | `--system` |
|---|---|---|
| Linux | `~/.config/systemd/user/citar-helper.service` (`Restart=on-failure`, `RestartSec=15`, `RestartPreventExitStatus=78`, `NoNewPrivileges=yes`), then `systemctl --user daemon-reload` and `enable --now`; it prints the `loginctl enable-linger` line for running without a login, and never runs it | `/etc/systemd/system/citar-helper.service` with a `User=` and the same restart rules; needs root, and says how rather than elevating |
| macOS | `~/Library/LaunchAgents/org.citar.helper.plist` (`RunAtLoad`, `KeepAlive` with `SuccessfulExit` false, `ThrottleInterval` 15, logs into the home), `launchctl bootstrap gui/<uid>` | not offered |
| Windows | a value `CITAR helper` under `HKCU\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run` running `citar-helper run --detach --home …`: `--detach` starts, with no window, a small **supervisor** that runs the helper as its child in service mode and starts it again at once after exit 75 and after a crash with backoff (15 s doubling to 10 minutes), and stops on 0; logs to the file | a Windows service (`windows-service`; `service run` is its entry), started automatically, running as the virtual account `NT SERVICE\citar-helper` (not LocalSystem: network-facing code that parses server data needs no more); `install --system` copies the binary into `%ProgramData%\CITAR\helper\bin` (so self-update can write it), sets the home `%ProgramData%\CITAR\helper` to a DACL of SYSTEM, Administrators and that account only with inheritance from `%ProgramData%` off (which would give Users read of `helper.json` and `machine-key.json`), and sets failure actions (restart after 15 s, with `fFailureActionsOnNonCrashFailures` so a non-zero exit such as 75 counts); installing needs an elevated prompt, and an unelevated `install --system` prints the elevated command and exits 2 |

A service's restart after exit 75 is the update's restart (P4.3.8). On this laptop only `--dry-run` runs on Windows, in a test that first checks the process is not elevated and aborts if it is; real installs run in WSL with `systemd --user` (P4.7.1); the Windows service and launchd install for real in CI only (P4.7.10).

### P4.3.10 What the helper never does

It never runs a command, a script or code it was sent; never reads or writes a file outside its home (and the service files it was asked to write); never dials anything but its server, its configured model endpoint and, for an update, the signed manifest's asset URL and the `https` redirects it leads to (the file is trusted by its signed hash, not by its host); never loads, unloads or swaps a model; never changes a system setting. It runs only the engine and bots compiled into it, on specs that are data (P4.4.1). These are the claims `WORKERS.md` makes to a lender and `SECURITY.md` defends (P4.6).

**As built in 4-03: the Rust helper's configuration, connection, keys, settings and catalog** (P4.3.2, P4.3.3, P4.3.7 and the discovery half of P4.3.4; Appendix E). One agent, 2026-10-14, from 12:58 to 15:06 (-04:00), on branch `p-4-03` from `89826fa`. Rust changed (citar-jobs is new, the helper gains everything but the proxy, the scan, jobs, updates and services; xtask develop), plus one Python test module and its table. The hub, the Python worker and the scheduler are untouched: nothing on the server speaks protocol 2 until 4-05.

- **citar-jobs** holds only `sys` (P4.3.1): `restrict_to_owner` (mode 0600; on Windows a protected DACL granting the current user, SYSTEM and Administrators full control and nobody else anything, written through `SetNamedSecurityInfoW` from an SDDL built with the token's user SID) and `exposure` (who else can read a file: the group or others by its mode; on Windows every allow entry, inherited or not, for any other SID, an owner outside that set, or a NULL DACL, read through `GetNamedSecurityInfoW` and the ACEs themselves, never `icacls`), with the command that fixes it (`chmod 600 '…'`, or `icacls "…" /inheritance:r /grant:r *<user>:F *S-1-5-18:F *S-1-5-32-544:F /remove:g *<each explicit SID>`, which the keys test runs). The `unsafe` is in `src/sys/windows.rs` only, each block with its `// SAFETY:` comment; `src/sys/unix.rs` is safe code. The crate has its own relaxed clippy.toml (a host crate) and declares `test-jobs` for 4-08.
- **The helper** (`crates/citar-helper/src`, about 3,200 lines of code and 625 of unit tests): `cli` (clap; every protocol 1 flag with its meaning, long names abbreviable and the last of a repeated flag winning, as argparse; the flags global so they work before or after `run` or `setup`), `config` (`build_config`'s sources and order, and the new keys), `home` (the home, the dev guard's rule), `log` (stderr and `helper/logs/helper.log`, rotated at 5 MB with three kept, every secret replaced by `[redacted]`; a handle, not a global logger, so tests run several helpers in one process), `clock` (the wall clock; a fake one follows tokio's paused clock, reads local time as UTC and can jump), `quiet` (protocol 1's parser and the stretch in force: `until` follows windows that meet across midnight, quiet all week ends a week on), `key`, `llm` (discovery and the cap), `net` (the dialler: TCP, then rustls with ring and `rustls-platform-verifier` for `wss`, then the WebSocket handshake; the loopback guard on the URL before any lookup and again on every resolved address), `settings` (the acknowledgement), `session` (the connection), `app` (the command line's order of checks). Dependencies as P4.3.1 names them, each with its reason in the manifest, with two choices of this package: TLS on **ring**, not aws-lc-rs (no CMake or NASM on any target; the C toolchain zstd already needs), and **tokio-tungstenite 0.29**, not 0.30: 0.30's rand 0.10 turns on getrandom 0.4's `rand_core`, which the engine's and the bot's content codes reach through the lock (blake3's build dependency cc, then jobserver), so it moved the build id with no game changed, and citar-bot's lock test caught it. With 0.29 the build id is 4-01's, `0b412a18fe95`.
- **Decisions taken here:**
  1. **`hello.llm.provider` is the endpoint's kind unless `--no-llm`.** PROTOCOL.md said `null` "when not enabled"; until 4-04 `llm.enabled` is false (the scope), and `catalog-on-change` expects `"lmstudio"` there. PROTOCOL.md now says `null` with `--no-llm`, which has no endpoint; no schema changes.
  2. **`hello.cpu.enabled` is false until 4-10** (`session::RUNS_JOBS`; the hello has no jobs build, so no job is offered), while `settings_ack` follows the rules (`settings-capped` expects CPU work on when the site and the machine's switch both say so). Until 4-04 the acknowledgement never turns model work on (`session::SERVES_COMPLETIONS`), capping `llm.enabled` when the site asks: the transcripts the helper plays keep the site's model work off, so they pass before and after 4-04 flips it.
  3. **Limits are reported soft on every OS** (`settings::UNTIL_THE_JOB_HOST`) until 4-08's job host says what holds: nothing is claimed hard before it is.
  4. **Until 4-04 a `request` gets `error` `unknown_model`** (not retryable) and **until 4-10 a `job_offer` gets `job_reject` `build`**; a bad `request` whose id reads gets `bad_request`, a bad offer whose run reads `bad_spec`; a `cancel` for nothing in flight, `update`, `jobs_pause` and the rest are logged and ignored. A transcript written in `tests/conformance.rs` holds each.
  5. **Discovery:** LM Studio's embedding models are left out (they cannot chat); a model `openai` or `openai_compatible` lists counts as loaded (protocol 1 reported `loaded: false` for every non-LM Studio model, so the scheduler's loaded-model rule refused them unless swaps were allowed); with an allowlist only its models are offered, one the endpoint does not list as not loaded; the list sent is sorted by key, so an endpoint that reorders its list sends no catalog. A listing that fails reports no models (logged at most every 10 minutes), as protocol 1's hello did (the fix round below made that three listings in a row).
  6. **The API key** is the variable `api_key_env` names, else `OPENAI_API_KEY` for `openai` only, sent to the configured address only (protocol 1 sent `OPENAI_API_KEY` to any endpoint).
  7. **Exit codes:** 0 stopped (Ctrl-C, SIGTERM), 78 a permanent refusal from a console, 2 a command line, settings, home or key it refuses (argparse's code for usage errors; the dev guard's refusals included), 1 a failure to start. In service mode a permanent refusal idles an hour, logged once until a later welcome.
  8. **Settings values protocol 1 took and misread:** `max_concurrent` above 16 is taken as 16 with a warning (a hello may not declare more), a negative one is refused; a `models` string is one model (protocol 1 iterated its characters); a UTF-8 byte-order mark in a settings file is accepted; an unknown provider, or a `cpu` key outside its five, is refused. The new flags make three prefixes ambiguous that were not (`--serv`, `--no`, `--c`), refused as argparse refuses one.
  9. **Quiet hours** are looked at every second on the wall clock (which can be set, so no timer is trusted for them); `status` goes at most every 5 s, the first as soon as they begin.
  10. **`--version`** prints `jobs build none` until 4-07, and `, machine key <fingerprint>` when the home holds a key; a dev-guard build reads a home only when `--home` names it.

It drives the helper's own session in-process over an in-memory pipe on tokio's paused clock (`tests/conformance.rs`: the 31 transcripts in 0.03 s), or the real binary over loopback in real time (`tests/real_binary.rs`). - **Fix round** (one agent, 2026-10-14, from 15:23 to 16:37 (-04:00), on `p-4-03` from `d7b6d28`): the review's 8 findings (2 major, 6 minor) were each verified and each fixed, with a test that holds the fix. Four of the old behaviours planted back (the moving quiet answer, one failure enough for an empty catalog, redirects followed, a type quoted uncut) each failed their tests.
  1. **A hostile server's text is bounded** (threat A, P4.6). `log::clip` quotes text from elsewhere cut and with control characters escaped; every line is cut at 4,096 characters after its secrets are replaced; `once` and `throttled` each keep at most 256 keys and then share one (kept apart, so a flood of invented types never silences a throttled warning), and a line below the level records no key. The session quotes an unknown type cut to 64 characters, keys a bad frame by its known type or `?`, never parses a frame that failed the envelope (too large, too deep, not an object) a second time, and cuts refusal messages, shutdown reasons, close reasons and run lists to 300. `tests/session.rs` sends 1,000 distinct 8 KB types, ten of 1 MB, a 9 MB frame, a megabyte value the checks quote and a megabyte field name: the keys stay within 2 x 257, no line is over 2 KB, and the connection still answers a ping.
  2. **A failed listing is not a catalog change** (P4.2.3). The catalog is kept across connections (`session::Catalog`): a listing that fails or times out is logged (throttled) and changes nothing; only three in a row (`FAILED_LISTINGS`, 90 s at the poll) report the models as none (an allowlist's, not loaded), and the next answer reports them again; a reconnection whose own listing fails sends the last list in its hello. LM Studio's own API failing within 5 minutes of an answer (`llm::V0_FRESH`) is a hiccup: the OpenAI list is read with that answer's load states and contexts, its embedding models still left out. Decision 5's last sentence above is superseded; PROTOCOL.md's `catalog` says so.
  3. **Quiet hours all week** were reported as ending a week on, a time that moved every second (a `status` every 5 s, about 17,000 log lines a day). They are now reported as ending at the start (Monday 00:00, local) of the week after next: more than a week away, the same all week, so one `status` a week. Every other stretch's end was already a whole minute; a test plays `status-quiet-hours` with `quiet_until` bound to the next 06:00, and fails a minute off.
  4. **Every test feature carries the guard** (P4.3.1): `app::DEV_GUARD` is on for `dev-guard`, `test-faults` and `test-update-key`, each alone (a loopback server, an explicit `--home` outside the per-user base); the real binary's guard test runs in each such build, run here with `test-faults` alone and `test-update-key` alone, and in WSL with `test-faults` alone.
  5. **The endpoint client follows no redirect** (a 3xx is an endpoint error naming its `location`; a test's redirect target sees no request) and carries a connect timeout only, each listing its own 10 s; 4-04's proxy shares the client and gives each completion its own `timeout_s` (capped at 900 s).
  6. **A log another program holds open** (on Windows, without delete sharing) is moved aside first when it rotates, so a failure shifts and deletes no older log; rotation is retried a minute later, the file grows to 10 MB at most, and then lines go to standard error only. The size is read from the file at each open. Tested with a handle without delete sharing on Windows and a directory nobody may write on Unix.
  7. **`updates`** of any type but text is refused, as every new key is (`"updates": false` used to mean `auto`).
  8. **Every wait is bounded** (P4.7.4): the close frames the helper sends have a 2 s timeout; the listing and the hardware file before each connection race the stop signal; the hardware file is read with a 5 s timeout (`HARDWARE_READ`), and a `rescan` reads it off the session's task, the `hardware` frame going from the session's loop when it is done. Tests: a stop while the endpoint never answers ends the helper at once (not after the listing's 10 s), and a hardware "file" that never answers (a named pipe on Windows, a FIFO elsewhere) delays the hello by its 5 s and holds up no ping after a `rescan`. S9's first run caught that test itself hanging in WSL (the FIFO was deleted before the writer that ends its blocked reads could open it, so the runtime's shutdown waited on them); fixed, and five more runs there pass.

Every helper run had its own home and dialled loopback only. **CI-only, not run:** test.yml on its three OSes; rust.yml (the helper's tests on Windows, Linux and macOS arm64, where `tests/session.rs` takes the FIFO and the log's held-file test the read-only directory); determinism.yml (no engine change, the build id unchanged).

**As built in 4-04: the Rust helper's LLM proxy and real cancel** (P4.3.4, P4.2.4; Appendix E). One agent, 2026-10-14, from 17:39 to 19:27 (-04:00), on branch `p-4-04` from `c916328`. Rust (the helper crate; two workspace dependencies already in the lock), `PROTOCOL.md` and `CONTRIBUTING.md`; the hub, the Python worker and the scheduler are untouched: nothing on the server speaks protocol 2 until 4-05.

- **The proxy** (`src/proxy.rs`, about 780 lines besides its unit tests; `src/http1.rs`, 280; the session's part, 250). Each `request` that local policy lets through (in order: `unknown_model` for `--no-llm` or a model outside the allowlist, `closed` in the local quiet hours, `busy` past `max_concurrent`) runs as a task of its own in the connection's `Flights` (a `JoinSet` with the ids, start times and token counts). The body is the request's model, messages and tools as they came (an empty tool list left out), the five parameters (any other dropped, each name logged once) and the helper's own `stream` with `stream_options.include_usage`; it goes to `<base>/chat/completions` on all four kinds, with the API key as a bearer token to the configured address only. A streamed answer is read from its server-sent events (a bounded WHATWG reader) to `data: [DONE]` and assembled raw: content and reasoning (`reasoning_content` or `reasoning`) concatenated, tool calls by `index` (the first id and type kept, name and arguments concatenated), the last finish reason and the last usage with both counts; only choice 0. `done` carries `gen_s` and `ttft_s` from `Instant`. **Cancel** aborts the task; `cancelled` goes once the task is gone, with `gen_s` and the events that carried text (`tokens_out`); a cancel that crosses a finished answer is ignored and the `done` or `error` is the one answer. **A lost connection** stops every completion before the next connection (`Flights::stop`, bounded at 2 s, besides the `JoinSet`'s own drop), so the next requests are never `busy` for a dead one; **a stop** (Ctrl-C, the service manager) answers each completion `error` `stopping`, retryable, before the close. `timeout_s` is held to 900 s; past it the completion is dropped, its connection with it, and answered `timeout`. `status` now also goes when the completions in flight change (at most every 5 s). The hello says `llm.enabled` unless `--no-llm`; `session::SERVES_COMPLETIONS` is gone.
- **Decisions taken here** (each written into `PROTOCOL.md`, "Completions" and "What the helper caps"):
  1. **Completions run on a connection the request's own task dials and drives** (`src/http1.rs`: hyper's HTTP/1 client connection over the helper's own TCP or rustls stream, with `hyper` and `hyper-util` named directly, both already in the lock through reqwest with these features), not on reqwest's pooled client: the close happens in the task's drop, so "the connection closed, and only then `cancelled`" holds by construction, and there is no pool to keep or reuse a cancelled generation's connection. Measured here, reqwest's pool also closed a dropped stream's connection promptly (0.27 ms after the drop, against 0.12 ms for the owned one, in a throwaway experiment), so the reason is the order and the absence of pooled state, not a pool that would leave generations running. Listings stay on 4-03's reqwest client. (The fix round keeps a finished answer's connection for the next completion, never a cancelled or failed one's: its fix 5.)
  2. **`ttft_s` is `null` for an unstreamed answer** (its first token is not seen apart from the rest), and for a streamed one with no token.
  3. **Content and reasoning are `null` when no delta carried them,** and `""` when one carried `""` (the endpoint's own words); a call with no `type` is a `function` call; a tool-call delta with no `index` starts a new call when it has an id and adds to the last otherwise; an unstreamed message's calls are placed by their position; arguments given as JSON other than a string are written out as text.
  4. **What a `done` carries is cut to the protocol** (512 calls, ids and names 200 characters, types 32, finish reasons 64; each kind of cut logged once), so a correct helper never sends a `done` the hub refuses; an answer whose text passes 8 MiB is dropped as it streams, and one whose `done` would be larger than a frame once JSON escapes it is not sent: both are `error` `endpoint`, not retryable.
  5. **`endpoint` is retryable** when the endpoint could not be reached or its connection broke, and for HTTP 408, 425, 429 and 5xx; not for any other status, an answer that does not read, an error the endpoint reported in its answer or stream, or one too large. The message quotes the endpoint's first 300 characters, **with the helper's secrets (its API key) replaced first** (`Log::redact`), since an endpoint can echo the key it was sent and the message goes to the server. An error's body is read only to its first 16 KiB, within 10 s.
  6. **The streaming fallback:** a streaming request with tools refused with HTTP 400, 422, 500 or 501 whose body names streaming and tools (or functions) is asked again unstreamed, and requests with tools stay unstreamed for the rest of that connection to the server (logged once: such a cancel may not stop the model); an endpoint that answers JSON to a streaming request is read as unstreamed (`streamed` false).
  7. **A `request` whose id is already in flight is ignored** (logged, throttled): one answer per id.
- **The transcript player** (`tests/common`) gains `FakeEndpoint`, the fake model endpoint over real HTTP on loopback: it lists models as LM Studio does, answers each completion with the behaviour its transcript scripts (server-sent events with a delay before the first and between the others, chunked; JSON; an HTTP error; no answer), and records each request's head and body, each event's time (taken while its client is known to be there) and when its client went (a read of the closed socket). The player plays every endpoint step (`respond` waits until the request it answers has come, so a cancel cannot overtake its own request), and the helper state `in_flight` (the tasks alive, waited for up to 2 s). The 14 completion transcripts run in real time, side by side, against the helper's session in-process and the fake endpoint (a paused clock would fire the helper's timers while the endpoint's bytes are on their way); the hello's model list comes from the transcript, since LM Studio's own API has no labels.
- **Gates.** (1) **The transcripts:** all 45 the helper plays (4-03's 31 on the paused clock and 4-04's 14 in real time) pass in-process in 4.8 s within the workspace run (4.1 s alone) (`tests/conformance.rs`); five of the 14 (`completion-streamed-tool-call`, `cancel-mid-stream`, `completion-timeout`, `completion-not-streamed`, `completion-params-whitelisted`) pass against the real binary too (`tests/real_binary.rs`). (2) **Cancel at the endpoint** (`tests/proxy.rs`): a 50-word stream at 100 ms a word cancelled after a second: the endpoint's client gone within 1 s of the `cancel`, no event after it, `cancelled` within 1 s with between one and the events sent (at most two in flight); and a cancel while the model is thinking (headers sent, the first event 30 s away) and in a 30 s pause between two events: gone within 1 s, `tokens_out` 0 and 1. (3) **Parameters:** five reach the endpoint with the helper's own streaming whatever the request says (`stream: false`, `base_url`, `api_key`, `model` and `logit_bias` in `params` dropped), each other name logged once over two requests. Besides: the streaming fallback, JSON to a streaming request, HTTP 503, 429 and 400 (a 1,000-character body quoted to 300), an error inside a stream and in a JSON answer, an answer with no choice, no endpoint at all (retryable), an answer past 8 MiB as text and one only as escaped JSON, two slots and a third `busy`, a repeated id answered once, a stop answering `stopping`, `status` following the completions in flight, the 900 s cap (paused clock), and the API key at the endpoint and `[redacted]` in the server's error. **Planted bugs:** 11 of 12 were caught (`C:/dev/target/p-4-04-mutate.log`): a cancel that does not abort, a lost connection that leaves its completions running, tokens not counted, names not concatenated, other parameters reaching the endpoint, the event reader rescanning from the start (the quadratic it had before this package's own fix), no redaction, `busy` one late, no 900 s cap, no streaming fallback, a `done` too large sent anyway; the twelfth (the connection driven by a task of its own) was not a fault, as decision 1's measurement shows. The test, run once with the opt-in, refused with nothing sent to the model (its only request was the model list): its guard works. Ten bugs planted back one at a time each failed their tests (`C:/dev/target/p-4-04-fix-mutate.log`): addresses dialled one after another, the answering address forgotten, no connection kept, an HTTP error's connection kept, a cut-off request not sent again, a kept connection used unchecked, a cancel that aborts half a second late, `endpoint` for `stopping`, a stop that closes without answering, and a finished connection's untaken request awaited.
  1. **A host's addresses are raced** (the major finding, verified: on this laptop `localhost` resolves to `::1` and then `127.0.0.1`, a refused `::1` takes about 2.04 s, and with the old sequential dial planted back the first completion to an endpoint listening on `127.0.0.1` only took 2.02 s from request to `done`). `net::race` tries a name's addresses as RFC 8305 says: the families alternating from the resolver's first choice, each next attempt 250 ms after the one before or at once when one fails, the first connection winning and the others dropped, all within the caller's connect budget; `net::Connector` remembers the address that answered and tries it first next time. Both the endpoint's completions (`http1`) and the WebSocket dialler use it. Measured here (`tests/proxy.rs`, the helper given `http://localhost:<port>/v1`): the first completion 0.26 s from request to `done`, the next 3 ms. A connect failure names the host and the address that failed. `gen_s` and `ttft_s` now count from sending the request that answered, once its connection is made (P4.2.4's words; they counted the dialling before, so the 2 s showed in `gen_s` too).
  2. **No event after the cancel, measured from the cancel** (verified: the fake endpoint stamps an event only while its client is there, so no event could ever be later than its own record of the disconnect). `tests/proxy.rs` counts the events stamped after the `cancel` was sent: at a word every 100 ms at most the one on its way may pass. The transcript step is `{"expect_no_tokens_after": "cancel"}` now (`cancel-mid-stream`, PROTOCOL.md's endpoint steps): the player keeps the time it sent the last `cancel`. A cancel planted to abort half a second late kept the old checks green (client gone within 1 s, `cancelled` within 1 s) and failed the new ones (5 events after the cancel in `tests/proxy.rs`, the transcript too).
  3. **`stop-answers-stopping`** (verified: the promise was in PROTOCOL.md and tested on the helper's side only): a request in flight on an endpoint that does not answer, the helper told to stop (the new act word `stop`, in PROTOCOL.md and both loaders; the in-process player sets the helper's stop signal), `error` `stopping` retryable within 2 s, a close with 1000, its endpoint connection gone, nothing in flight, exit 0 without reconnecting; the hub's side checks the request `requeued` (4-05 plays it). The fixtures are 88 transcripts now; the helper's side plays 46 (4-03's 31 and 4-04's 15), the hub's side 40 from 4-05.
  4. **The real-model test is ignored unless asked for** (verified: without the variable it returned early and counted as passed): `#[ignore]`, so other runs report it skipped; `CITAR_TEST_LMSTUDIO=http://localhost:1234 cargo nextest run -p citar-helper --features citar-helper/dev-guard --run-ignored only -E 'binary(real_model)' --no-capture` runs it, and asked for without the variable it fails saying so (`CONTRIBUTING.md`).
  5. **A finished answer's connection carries the next completion** (verified: every completion dialled a new connection, and an https endpoint a new TLS handshake, two round trips to a remote endpoint, where the design's reqwest would have kept the connection). `http1::Pool` keeps a connection whose answer was read whole, with the endpoint keeping it open, at most 8 and at most 30 s idle (under the idle timeouts of NAT boxes, which drop a connection without a word); after a streamed answer's `[DONE]` the body's last bytes are read at once when they are there, else by a task of their own within 1 s. A cancelled, timed-out or failed completion's connection is still closed in its drop and never kept, so cancel is unchanged. A kept connection is driven before it is used (an endpoint's close that came while it waited ends it there), and a request that fails on one within 1 s goes once more on a new connection (the endpoint closed it as the request came; a completion changes nothing at the endpoint). Tests: a streamed and an unstreamed answer on one connection; a cancelled stream's and an HTTP 503's connection closed and not kept; a kept connection the endpoint closed is not used; a request cut off on a kept connection is sent again, the same body, on a new one, while one cut off on a new connection is a retryable `endpoint` error sent once. On a loopback endpoint the saving is nothing measurable (a connect costs well under a millisecond); for a remote https endpoint it is the TCP and TLS round trips, computed, not measured here (no TLS test server without a new dependency or a committed key).
  6. **Found on the way:** with the look before reuse planted away, a kept connection the endpoint had already closed held its next request for the completion's whole timeout: hyper reads the close while the connection is idle, finishes it cleanly and leaves the request queued. Behind the look that is a race of microseconds, but a wait of up to 900 s is not a risk to keep: a connection that finishes with no response ready for its request is now the endpoint's failure at once (retryable, and sent again when the connection was kept). Two `http1` unit tests hold it, the kept connection's case waiting 2 s and failing before the fix.
